| Follow-up step | change set `applying`, step `running` | automatic resume by probe (1.5) |
| Index build waiting on another session | step `running`, watchdog report | end the blocking session, or `std schema abandon <id>` |
| Repeatedly failing step | `needs_attention` | fix the cause and `std schema resume <id>`, or `std schema abandon <id>` |
| `std upgrade` | stopped at a boundary | rerun `std upgrade` |

`std schema status` shows open change sets, steps, last error, build progress and the `SCHEMA_KEY` holder's PID and client address. The v0.1 docs include a runbook with exact commands for each row, executed by CI.

### 8.8 Rolling upgrades (draft design)

**Status: Draft, owned by Step 5 (5B, 5O); binding now: RU1–RU12's rules for code written from Step 3, see decision 0026's "Step 3 obligations from 8.8"; open issues below.**

Martin decided on 2026-10-22 that rolling upgrades are a v0.1 goal (decision 0021, decision 51): during an upgrade from release N to N+1, servers and workers of both versions run side by side against one database, and no request fails because of the mix. This reverses open question 2's offline default and 13.1's `Recreate` lifecycle. **This section is a design for review**; the rules RU1 to RU12 bind every migration, set version and delivery written from Step 3 on, so that Step 5 does not have to rewrite them. The offline path of 8.5 stays as the simple case (no server connected: every phase runs at once) and for the quickstart and Docker compose.

**Phases.** An upgrade from N to N+1 has three phases, each a mode of `std upgrade`:

1. **Expand** (`std upgrade --expand`), while release N serves: platform migrations of phase `expand`, resumption of interrupted change sets, and the additive part of each set version (new tables, fields that are optional or have a default, choices, indexes, definitions release N ignores). After it, release N keeps working unchanged and release N+1 can start.
2. **Roll**: N's servers and workers are replaced by N+1's one by one (Kubernetes `RollingUpdate`, 13.1). Both versions serve, and both behave as release N's finalized configuration says (RU7, RU9, RU12).
3. **Finalize** (`std upgrade --finalize`), after the fence below: the behavior part of each set version (record rules, access rules, required fields, retirements, removed choices), `data` migrations, the activation of release N+1's features, and the `contract` migrations that release N+1 ships for what an earlier release expanded.

`std upgrade` without a phase runs all three when no server or worker is connected, as today (8.5).

**Durable phase ordering.** One platform row, `core.upgrade_state`, holds the upgrade in progress: `phase` (`idle`, `expanding`, `expanded`, `finalizing`), the attempt ID, `from_version`, `to_version` and `min_admitted_version`; `core.release_attempt` keeps one row per completed phase of each attempt. Each phase takes the row `FOR UPDATE` and moves it in one transaction: expand starts only from `idle` (or resumes its own attempt's `expanding`), finalize only from `expanded` of the same attempt, and an attempt that a newer attempt superseded stops. So finalize can never run before its attempt's expand has committed, and two attempts never interleave. **Interrupted finalize.** Finalize records each completed step in `core.release_attempt` (fence raised, old sessions gone, each set's behavior part, each `data` migration, each `contract` migration, features activated), each in the transaction that does the work. From `finalizing`, the same attempt resumes after its last recorded step; a newer attempt for the same `to_version` (a rerun `helm upgrade`) takes the row over in one transaction, writing its own attempt ID and keeping the recorded steps, and continues from there, while the older attempt, finding the row no longer its own, stops; an attempt for another version is refused until the row is `idle`. Abort is refused once the fence is raised, so after the fence the way forward is always a resume or a takeover, never a way back [judgment].

**The admission fence.** Every server, worker and CLI process runs `core.admit('<its version>', '<role>')` in the first transaction of every new connection (the pool's connection setup, so also on every reconnect) and before it starts, and exits if it raises. For the `serve` and `worker` roles it raises unless the version is at least `min_admitted_version` and its own release's expand is applied. For the control commands (`upgrade` in every mode, `--abort` included, `wait-upgraded` and `doctor`) it enforces only the floor, the version at least `min_admitted_version`, and not the phase the command performs or waits for, so release N+1's `std upgrade --expand` and `std wait-upgraded` can run before N+1's expand exists, and no release older than the fence can run any of them. Before `core.admit` exists in a database (the first upgrade that creates it), a control command proceeds and the expand creates it. Finalize works in this order:

1. wait until this attempt's expand row exists and the rollout of this attempt's pod template is complete: the finalize Job reads the Deployments through the Kubernetes API (a ServiceAccount allowed to get and list Deployments, ReplicaSets and Pods) and requires every pod to carry this attempt's template, and no pod of an older template to exist (offline and outside Kubernetes, `--wait-for-old` waits for the sessions instead);
2. in one transaction, set `min_admitted_version` to N+1 and `phase` to `finalizing` (the fence);
3. terminate every session whose `application_name` names an older version (RU10) and wait until none remains; any old process that reconnects now fails `core.admit` and exits;
4. only then run the finalize work, and set `phase` to `idle` with `from_version` N+1.

Because the fence commits before the old sessions are terminated and every new connection checks it, no old process can reconnect after finalize work starts, however its connections come and go.

**Going back before finalize.** Before step 2 of finalize, the previous release's chart can be applied again: `std upgrade --abort --attempt <ID>` of release N (the previous chart's Job) sets the row back to `idle` at version N; release N+1's expand migrations and the additive parts of its set versions stay (RU2 makes them harmless to N), recorded as `expanded, abandoned` so a later upgrade applies them again idempotently. 8.4's refusal of downgrades applies only to set versions whose behavior part was finalized: a set version that is only expanded is not a downgrade for the older CLI, which leaves it alone. Every delivery written during the roll is processable by release N workers (RU9), so nothing waits for a worker that no longer exists. After finalize, going back is a restore and a `helm upgrade`, as 13.1 says.

**Rules for every migration, set version and delivery** (they bind Step 3's work now: 3A, 3B, 3P, 3I and every later one):

- RU1. Every platform migration declares its phase, `expand` or `contract`, besides its kind (`tx`, `concurrent_index`, `data`); the ledger records it (`core.platform_migration.phase`, added by 0017; migrations 0001–0016 read as `expand`, with original checksum inputs preserved — **Step 3 precision, decision 0030**). `data` migrations always run in finalize.
- RU2. An `expand` migration leaves the database readable and writable by the previous release's code: it may create tables, functions, types and indexes (`CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY`), add columns that are nullable or have a constant default, add enum values and widen a check; it may not drop, rename or change the type of anything the previous release uses, add `NOT NULL` or a constraint that the previous release's writes could violate, change what an existing column means, or change the signature or behavior of a function the previous release calls (`core.begin_request()` and the rest get a new name instead, such as `core.begin_request_v2()`).
- RU3. A rename or a type change goes through three releases' worth of steps, and the old storage stays authoritative until finalize: expand adds the new storage; during the roll both releases read and write the old storage, and release N+1 also writes the new storage but never reads it; finalize's `data` migration copies old into new for every record (synchronization) and then activates release N+1's reads and writes of the new storage through the fence; a `contract` migration at least one release later drops the old storage. So a write by either release is never lost or read stale.
- RU4. A `contract` migration removes only what neither the release it ships in nor the release before it reads or writes, and ships at least one release after the expand that replaced it.
- RU5. Expand DDL follows 1.4's lock budget and never rewrites a large table (no `ALTER COLUMN TYPE`, no volatile default on an added column); long work is a `concurrent_index` or a batched `data` migration.
- RU6. A `data` migration runs in finalize, is batched and idempotent, and keeps 1.9's ledger contract (`state` `running` until its `remaining` count proves nothing is left, `progress` with the cursor; decision 0024), selecting again until nothing needs the change, so records written during the roll are covered. Until it finishes, release N+1 treats a record the migration has not reached as valid: it reads, writes, evaluates rules on and renders events for it exactly as for a migrated one, never fails on it, and never reads the value the migration would produce (for the party backfill, 3P: a user without an individual is read and written normally, and gets one on its next save or from the migration).
- RU7. A shipped set version has an additive part, applied in expand, and a behavior part (record rules, access rules, required fields, retirements, removed choices), applied in finalize. **Record rules in particular take effect only in finalize**: during the roll every admitted release runs the same, previously finalized rules, so a save through an old pod and one through a new pod give the same result. A release may not depend on its own new behavior before it is finalized.
- RU8. Wire compatibility both ways during the roll: release N+1 accepts release N's requests (already required, 8.5 release compatibility), and records, fields and choices that release N+1 creates are valid for release N, which reads them through its catalog like any local addition.
- RU9. Deliveries (3B): until finalize, release N+1 produces only deliveries a release N worker can process (event kinds, payload formats and delivery columns release N knows); each delivery row records `min_worker_version`, set to the oldest admitted version until finalize activates N+1's new kinds; a worker never claims a row above its own version. **Step 3 precision (decision 0030):** the offline producer currently writes the package version; comparison is numeric `major.minor.patch`, and too-new rows remain in the ordering anti-join. The oldest-admitted floor policy waits for Step 5 admission state. So going back before finalize never strands a delivery, and per-record order (4.7) is never blocked by a row no admitted worker can claim.
- RU10. Servers and workers set `application_name` to `std-server@<version>` and `std-worker@<version>` (the CLI to `std-cli@<version>`), so finalize can tell versions apart; 8.5's checks match the prefix. **Step 3 precision (decision 0030):** names come from the canonical release version in `packages/server/package.json` (currently 0.0.3); coordinator and resumer connections use the same versioned naming helper; checks match the `@` boundary and also the exact legacy unversioned names.
- RU11. **New kinds take effect only when every admitted process knows them.** Any new definition kind, config kind, dependency kind, config feature (an operator, field type, rule action or setting) or subscription kind is usable only once every admitted process runs the release that introduced it: until finalize raises `min_admitted_version` to that release, no process creates it, and a config commit (change set, definition write, set load, TMF672 write) that would use one is refused with `DefinitionInvalid` problem `upgradeInProgress`, and succeeds after finalize. Each such feature is tagged in `@std/definitions` with the release that introduced it. Examples from Step 3: 3A's config kind `recordRule` (the previous release's catalog loader stops on a `core.config_base` row of a kind it does not know), and 3B's config subscriptions on `core.role` and `core.access_rule` (the previous release re-validates every definition on each change set and refuses subscriptions on platform tables, so it would refuse every change set while one existed).
- RU12. **Tolerance ships one release before first use.** A release's catalog loader skips config rows of kinds it does not know (`core.config_base` and the other config tables) and logs them once; its change-set validation re-validates only definitions whose kinds and features it knows and accepts the others as they are; its dependency index ignores dependency kinds it does not know; and its `doctor` reports a newer release's unknown config kinds, dependency kinds, subscription kinds and platform tables as information, not findings. A new kind of RU11 may first be used only by a release whose previous release already carries this tolerance. **Step 3 precision (decision 0030):** rebuilding known edges preserves unknown kinds, and unknown base rows' overrides and opaque hashes are left untouched. v0.0.3 ships config/dependency-kind tolerance, with unknown kinds logged once per load. Unknown field types, opaque preservation of newer properties on known kinds, and informational unknown subscription-kind diagnostics are deferred until before their first mixed-release use; this is not yet full RU12 tolerance. Step 3's own upgrade from `v0.0.2` stays offline, so its new kinds (3A's `recordRule`, dependency kinds `rule_on` and `rule_writes`, table `core.record_rule`; 3B's config subscriptions) need no tolerance in `v0.0.2`. Once Step 5's mechanics exist, a rolling upgrade from `v0.0.3` is possible only to a release that introduces no new field type, property or subscription kind, because `v0.0.3` does not carry that tolerance.

**What the startup check becomes** (replacing 1.9's last two bullets in Step 5, in the one place that decides it today, `refusalOf` in `platform/ledger.ts`, decision 0024): a process of release V starts when `core.admit(V)` passes, every `expand` migration of V is applied, every applied migration's checksum matches, and every ledger row it does not know is an `expand` migration of a later release. It refuses when a `contract` migration it does not know is applied, when an `expand` migration of its own release is missing, or when a checksum differs. Pending or `running` `data` migrations and a pending finalize of its own release do not stop it; `doctor` reports them as warnings, and as errors after a configurable time (default 24 h).

**Helm lifecycle** (13.1, Step 5): Deployments use `RollingUpdate` with `maxUnavailable: 0` and readiness probes. Per attempt, the expand Job (`std-upgrade-<attempt>`) runs `std upgrade --expand --attempt <ID>` while the old pods keep serving, and the new pods' init containers wait for that attempt's expand row (`std wait-upgraded --phase expand --attempt <ID>`). The finalize Job (`std-finalize-<attempt>`), created with everything else, waits for its attempt's expand row and the completed rollout (above), fences, and finalizes. Helm waits for nothing that waits on Helm, so `--wait` and `--wait-for-jobs` cannot deadlock. A failed expand leaves the old pods serving and the new ones in their init containers; a failed finalize after the fence leaves the new version serving with `doctor` reporting the pending finalize; rerunning `helm upgrade` is a new attempt that takes the finalize over from its last recorded step (durable phase ordering, above).

**Contract cleanup follow-up.** After event lookup backfill 0022 is applied everywhere and older writers are fenced out, a contract migration drops the temporary 0025/0026 lookup indexes and the point-lookup fallback code and ledger probe (decision 0032).

**Which step builds what.** Step 3: RU1 to RU12 bind every migration, set version and delivery written (3A, 3B, 3P, 3I), `v0.0.3` ships RU12's config/dependency-kind tolerance (known-kind feature and subscription-kind gaps are recorded in 0030), and the `migration-phases` check (workstream 3R) enforces RU1, RU2 and RU5 statically on every PR. Step 4: every migration still follows them. Step 5 (5B, with 5O for the chart): `core.upgrade_state`, `core.admit` and the fence, the three phases and `--abort` in `std upgrade`, the new startup check, RU11's release tags and refusal, `std wait-upgraded --phase`, the finalize Job and `RollingUpdate`.

**Tests.** `migration-phases` (Step 3, every PR): every platform migration declares a phase. **Precision (decision 0030, J2):** tooling parses each SQL string with pinned `libpg-query` 18.1.5 (PostgreSQL 18.4 grammar), requires exactly one parser statement in expand and contract, and refuses parse failures. Expand admits only the closed AST allow-list in J2: additive table/column/constraint changes, appropriate indexes and triggers, new parsed SQL/PLpgSQL invoker functions qualified in the STD-owned `core` schema, without SET or direct writes/dynamic SQL, empty `core` schemas, enums and comments. Existing-table added columns require allow-listed built-in types; existing-table FK actions must be NO ACTION or RESTRICT. All `concurrent_index` statements must be single concurrent index creations, in either phase. `core` must remain outside the application search_path (decision 0030). Every ALTER subcommand is checked. Exact historical exceptions remain frozen; contract SQL has no allow-list. This proves a syntactic policy, not expression volatility after catalog/type resolution, called-function semantics, domain types with constraints or defaults where still allowed (such as new tables), table size or lock duration, data-migration correctness or old-client compatibility. The executable canaries assert exact findings and accepted cases; the runtime retains `standard_conforming_strings = on`. `expand-compat` (Step 5): the previous release's API, record and outbox tests pass against a database that this release's `std upgrade --expand` has expanded. `rolling-upgrade` (Step 5): a load generator runs the previous release's and this release's quickstart requests while expand, a one-by-one roll of three servers and two workers, and finalize run; no request fails except connection resets the client retries, every committed change reaches its subscriptions once per `eventId`, per-record order holds, and `doctor` exits 0 at the end. `upgrade-fence` (Step 5): an old process whose connections are dropped after the fence cannot reconnect (`core.admit` refuses) and exits; finalize does not start before its attempt's expand row and the completed rollout; a second attempt cannot finalize the first's expand; N+1's `std upgrade --expand` and `std wait-upgraded` run before N+1's expand exists, and an N `std upgrade` after the fence is refused; a finalize killed right after the fence resumes from its recorded steps in the same attempt, and in a separate run a newer attempt for the same version takes it over and completes it, with each step done once. `rolling-alternating-writes` (Step 5): during a roll with a renamed field (RU3), alternating writes to one record through a release N and a release N+1 server each read back the latest value on both versions, and after finalize the new storage holds the last write. `rolling-abort` (Step 5): going back before finalize leaves no delivery unclaimed and the older CLI accepts the expanded set versions. `rolling-config` (Step 5): during the roll a change set using a feature only release N+1 knows (a record rule, a config subscription on `core.role`) is refused with `upgradeInProgress` and succeeds after finalize; the previous release keeps loading the catalog and accepting change sets while rows of newer kinds exist (RU12). `oda-helm-lifecycle` (13.1) gains the rolling cases: old pods serve through a failed expand; finalize waits for the rollout and the fence; going back before finalize needs no restore.

**Open issues** (not solved; for 5B and 5O):

- P1. A replacement Helm attempt cannot reach takeover. Expand runs only from `idle` or its own `expanding`, and the replacement attempt's pods and finalize Job wait for its own expand row, so after attempt A dies in `finalizing`, attempt B can neither expand, finish its rollout, nor take over. Needed: B adopts A's completed expand and recorded steps, writes its own readiness record, and nothing waits in a circle; a test of the real replacement Helm lifecycle.
- P1. Concurrent takeover has no enforceable ownership rule. Finalize spans several transactions: A can check ownership, B take over, and A run another step, unless ownership is checked under the row lock inside each step's work and checkpoint transaction; and "newer" has no order, because attempt IDs are UUIDv4 or caller-provided. Needed: attempt ordering assigned by the database, and per-step transactional fencing; a test of overlapping attempts, including a delayed older Job.

### 8.9 Transport security and signed releases (Step 3)

Martin decided on 2026-10-22 that both come now, in Step 3 (decision 0021, decision 50; GB1008 v1.1.0 §5.2.2 and §5.2.9 ask for protected data in transit and signed deliverables):

- **TLS to Postgres.** The server, worker and CLI connect to any database host other than loopback or a Unix socket with TLS and full verification (`sslmode=verify-full`: the CA from `STD_DATABASE_CA_FILE`, else the system store, and the host name checked against the certificate). A weaker mode for a remote host is refused at startup unless `STD_DATABASE_TLS=insecure` is set, which `doctor` reports as an error-level finding. Loopback connections (development, the quickstart, CI) may stay plaintext. The ODA chart's bundled Postgres serves TLS with a certificate from cert-manager, verified against the chart's CA; an external database needs `verify-full`.
- **Signed releases.** The release workflow builds the image from the tagged commit, pushes it to ghcr.io and signs it by digest with Sigstore cosign, keyless, with the workflow's GitHub OIDC identity, and attaches an SBOM and SLSA build provenance; it then verifies the signature against that identity and fails the release if verification fails. The Helm chart is packaged as an OCI artifact and signed the same way from its first release (Step 5, and the Step 3 Canvas probe's stub chart). The install docs show the `cosign verify` command with the expected identity.
- Tests: `db-tls` (a Postgres 18 with a test CA: `verify-full` connects; a wrong host name, an untrusted CA and a plaintext remote connection are refused; `STD_DATABASE_TLS=insecure` connects and `doctor` reports it); `release-signature` (on a release candidate tag, the workflow's verification step passes for the pushed digest and fails for an unsigned or differently signed digest).

### Acceptance tests

- Loading a set with shuffled files produces byte-identical SQL and identical IDs.
- A set defining a child before its parent, with two mutually referencing tables, loads; an `extends` cycle is refused and nothing applies.
- Fixture v1 to v2 with local label overrides on X and W: v2 changes X, sets W to the local value, changes Z (not overridden), renames a shipped role R, adds a role, and changes one access rule. Z shows v2; X keeps the local label with one conflict; W's override is gone with a `converged` entry; the access rule naming R keeps working; the access-rule and role changes appear under security changes.
- `take_shipped` on X sets the v2 label; `keep_local` survives a v3 that does not change X.
- Rerunning the same upgrade changes nothing, the revision included.
- v2 removing a field with data retires it with values intact; `adopt` moves it to `local`. v2 removing a role that a local access rule names is refused at plan time.
- A v2 that changes a type is refused at plan time; revision, catalog and config tables unchanged.
- Name clash: a v2 adding field `foo` to a shipped table where a local field `foo` exists is refused at plan time with a report naming the local field; after the admin renames it, the rerun succeeds and the local field's data and the rules reading it are intact.
- `--plan` leaves revision, catalog (schema-only dump diff) and config tables unchanged, and the real run's report equals the plan.
- `std upgrade` refuses to run while a `std-server` session is connected, and likewise while only a `std-worker` session is connected (`upgrade-refuses-worker`); with `--wait-for-disconnect` it proceeds once both are gone and refuses at the deadline otherwise; and it refuses on a non-UTF8 database.
- Release upgrade (Step 5): from the previous pre-release with helpdesk, 10,000 records with history and customizations (local field on a shipped table, overrides, a local class extending a shipped one, a local access rule, a local role), the candidate upgrade leaves `doctor` exiting 0, content checksums unchanged except for documented migrations, customizations effective, expected conflicts only.
- Backup and restore (Step 5): `pg_dump -Fc` under load during a follow-up phase, restored into an empty Postgres 18, then `std upgrade`: the change set completes, `doctor` exits 0, checksums match the snapshot, auto-numbers continue, sign-in works, a new change set succeeds.
- Restore then upgrade (Step 5): a previous pre-release backup reaches the same state as the release upgrade test.
- CI runs every runbook command against these scenarios.

## 9. Effect toolchain

- **Versions.** `effect` and the 4.x `@effect/*` runtime packages share one version and release in lockstep [checked: [Effect 4.0](https://effect.website/blog/releases/effect/40)]. STD pins them to one exact version (4.0.5 today) through a pnpm catalog, because `@effect/sql-pg` declares its peer as the range `effect: ^4.0.5`. `@effect/tsgo` has its own version line (0.54.1), releases several times a week, and is pinned exactly and upgraded in its own PR. `typescript` is pinned to a version in `@effect/tsgo`'s supported table (7.0.2).
- **Never installed:** `@effect/sql` and `@effect/platform`. They are Effect 3-era 0.x packages that still carry npm's `latest` tag; in v4 they live inside `effect` as `effect/sql` and `effect/http`.
- **Stability.** APIs tagged `@stability unstable` may break in minor releases, and `@stability experimental` ones in patch releases [checked: `repos/effect/MIGRATION.md` at `effect@4.0.5`]; since 4.0.2 every module declares its stability [checked: [Effect 4.0: The First Week](https://effect.website/blog/effect-4-first-week)]. In 4.0.5, all of `effect/sql`, `effect/http`, `effect/http-api`, `effect/rpc`, `effect/workflow`, `effect/cluster`, `effect/observability` and every module of `@effect/sql-pg`, `@effect/platform-node`, `@effect/opentelemetry` and `@effect/vitest` are unstable; `Effect`, `Layer`, `Schema`, `Stream` and most root modules are stable [checked: [effect-4.0.5.tgz](https://registry.npmjs.org/effect/-/effect-4.0.5.tgz)]. This includes `src/http-api/HttpApiClient.ts` and `src/http/HttpClient.ts` [checked: same tarball]. Unstable as whole modules in 4.0.5 are also the root modules `Arbitrary`, `ByteSize`, `ChannelSchema`, `Crypto`, `ErrorReporter`, `ExecutionPlan`, `FileSystem`, `Graph`, `HashRing`, `LayerMap`, `LayerRef`, `Newtype`, `PartitionedSemaphore`, `Path`, `PlatformError`, `Stdio`, `Terminal`, `TxChunk` and `Version`, and stable modules hold some unstable exports (`Schema` has 132 unstable and 46 experimental ones) [checked: `node_modules/effect/src`, 0A].
- **Diagnostics.** `@effect/tsgo` is the TypeScript 7 binary for editor and CI; TS 7.0 has no programmatic API, so tsserver plugins do not run on it [checked: [TypeScript 7.0](https://devblogs.microsoft.com/typescript/announcing-typescript-7-0/), [Effect-TS/tsgo](https://github.com/Effect-TS/tsgo)]. Setup: the tsconfig plugin entry is named `@effect/language-service`, and `"prepare": "effect-tsgo patch"` makes plain `tsc` report Effect diagnostics. Because `extends` replaces the whole `plugins` array, each package's own tsconfig (a copy of its profile's template in `tooling/tsconfig/`) sets `diagnosticSeverity` to error for `unstableApiUsage`, `experimentalApiUsage`, `outdatedApi`, `obsoleteSchemaImport`, `duplicatePackage` and `floatingEffect`, and keeps `ignoreEffectWarningsInTscExitCode: false`, so CI fails in the normal type-check step (`pnpm typecheck`, `tsc -p` per package). `tooling/check-tsconfig.ts` fails a package whose entry differs from its declared profile. The diagnostics fire where an unstable export is used, not on the import statement [checked: 0A canaries].
- **Patch and pnpm.** pnpm hard-links package files from its global store, so a patch that rewrote files in place would change the store copy used by other projects. `effect-tsgo patch` renames the store-linked `lib/tsc` to `lib/tsc.original` and writes a new, unshared `lib/tsc`; the store copy keeps its inode and content, so no `package-import-method` setting is needed [checked: canary `effect-tsgo-patch-and-pnpm`, 0A].
- **Allow lists**, kept in each package's own tsconfig. The entry strings below work as written; a module entry covers its submodules (`effect/sql` allows `effect/sql/SqlClient`), `module#export` allows one export, and `effect/http` does not cover `effect/http-api` [checked: 0A canaries, `docs/decisions/0001-effect.md`]:
  - Plain packages (definitions, values, conditions, access-rule evaluation): empty.
  - Shared API-definition package and web app: `allowedUnstableApis = ["effect/http-api", "effect/http"]`. Reason: the shared `HttpApi` definition and `HttpApiClient.make` live in these unstable modules.
  - Server package: `allowedUnstableApis = ["effect/sql", "effect/http", "effect/http-api", "effect/observability", "@effect/sql-pg", "@effect/platform-node", "@effect/opentelemetry"]`.
  - Tests, in every profile: an `overrides` entry for `"include": ["test/**/*"]` adding `@effect/vitest`, repeating the server entries because overrides replace the base list. `test/**` matches no file, and the glob resolves relative to the tsconfig that declares it [checked: 0A].
  - `allowedExperimentalApis` is empty everywhere. Unstable Schema exports are allowed one export at a time, only if needed.
  - Each entry has a one-line reason in the Effect decision note. Every Effect upgrade PR checks the API reference for changes in allowed modules and removes entries for modules promoted to stable.
- **"Effect Schema only" in plain packages.** Stability diagnostics flag only unstable imports, so a plain package importing stable `Effect`, `Layer` or `Stream` would pass them. A CI import check (`tooling/check-imports.ts`) allows plain packages' `src/` to import from `effect` only `Schema`, `SchemaAST`, `SchemaGetter`, `SchemaIssue`, `SchemaParser`, `SchemaTransformation`, `Option` and `Result`; the reasons are in the Effect decision note.
- **Later:** turn on `apiStabilityLeak` for STD's published packages once they tag their exports [unverified: that it follows tags into Effect's packages].
- **Agent material.** AGENTS.md carries a short idiom guide pointing at the diagnostics above and at `AGENTS.md` and `ai-docs/` shipped in the `effect` package. Effect's source is vendored at `repos/effect` (moved from `vendor/effect` in workstream 0A-foldkit) as a squashed subtree of the `effect@<version>` tag of `https://github.com/Effect-TS/effect` matching the lockfile, not `main` (the npm metadata has no `gitHead`; tag `effect@4.0.5` is commit `7f524e2`, tree `208c4ac`) [checked: 0A]. The CI check compares `HEAD:repos/effect` with the recorded tree hash, so the vendored files themselves must match the tag.
- **Measurement log.** Each Effect upgrade PR records engineer-hours and breakages per allowed module; defects record whether Effect was the root cause and which workstream they belong to; agent PRs record Effect-related rework. The plan's thresholds are reviewed at every stage gate.

### Acceptance tests

- A canary import of `effect/sql` in a plain package fails `tsc`; the same import in the server package passes.
- A canary import of `Effect` into a plain package fails the CI import check.
- The web app and the shared API-definition package type-check with `HttpApiClient.make`; an import of `effect/sql` in the web app fails.
- A second copy of `effect` in the lockfile fails CI (`duplicatePackage` plus a lockfile check).
- An Effect 3-era API in any package fails CI (`outdatedApi`).
- The vendored Effect source tag equals the lockfile version (CI check).

## 10. The API under TMF630 (Step 3)

Martin's requirement (2026-10-22, decision 0021): STD's own API follows the TMF630 REST API Design Guidelines. The verified baseline is the v5.0.0 text of Parts 1 and 4 to 8 (July 2026, Team Approved, pre-production) plus the v4.0.0 text of Parts 2 and 3, because v5 Parts 2 and 3 are unpublished (Martin, 2026-10-22; decision 0025). Every normative statement of it is a row of `docs/conformance/TMF630@5.0.0.yaml` (15); a rule below cites the part, section and page it rests on as **[checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §x p.N]** (or the TMF621 v5.0.1 documents, which the kits test). Where STD deviates on purpose, the rule says **[judgment]** with the reason, and the deviation is a matrix exclusion that Martin decides (15.3), or a contradiction decision 0025 reports to him. Each rule has a test in `tmf630-conformance`.

### 10.1 Versions and paths

- STD's API lives under `/api/v1`, its only API (Martin, 2026-10-23; decision 0021, decision 56: STD is greenfield and nobody used the Step 0 to 2 API, which is deleted). The major version is in the path, and minor versions never change it [checked: every v5 OpenAPI document serves `{apiRoot}/tmf-api/<apiName>/v5/`; TMF656 v5.1.0 still uses `/v5`]. The `/tmf-api` prefix is kept for TM Forum's own APIs (11.1) [judgment].
- Resource names follow v5 naming [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §2.5 p.22; Martin, 2026-10-22, M3]: URI segments lowerCamelCase, type names UpperCamelCase, no implementation details. A table is its own collection, `/api/v1/{collection}` and `/api/v1/{collection}/{id}`, where `{collection}` is its **collection name**: the namespace and table names in lowerCamelCase, joined with the table's first letter upper case (`helpdesk.ticket_note` is `helpdeskTicketNote`); its **type name** (`@type`, `@baseType`, `@referredType`, hub and event types) is the same in UpperCamelCase (`HelpdeskTicketNote`). The inverse is a lookup over the snapshot's live tables, never a parse of the name. Definition validation refuses (`DefinitionInvalid` `apiNameClash`) a table whose API name equals another live table's (`foo_bar.baz` and `foo.bar_baz`) or a fixed resource's (`session`, `tableMetadata`, `hub`, `listener`, `checkFormState`, `applyChangeSet`, `deadDelivery`, `retryDeadDelivery`, `discardDeadDelivery`, `openapi`), so a path segment names a fixed resource or a table, never both. Renaming a table or its namespace changes its API names like any rename: a request built before names a collection that no longer exists and gets `UnknownTable` (6.4 resolves the table at C first). The routes:

| Route | Resource |
| --- | --- |
| `GET`, `POST /api/v1/{collection}` | a table's records (`allowedFor` for pickers, 6.5) |
| `GET`, `PATCH`, `DELETE /api/v1/{collection}/{id}` | one record |
| `GET /api/v1/{collection}/{id}/history[/{changeId}]` | its `ChangeEntry` collection (10.4) |
| `POST /api/v1/checkFormState` | a task resource, not persisted: `200` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §9.1 p.138, §9.4 pp.146-148: task names are verbs] |
| `GET /api/v1/tableMetadata[/{collection}[/schema.json\|/openapi.json]]` | the tables, one table's metadata (6.3), its resource JSON Schema for the caller and its OpenAPI document |
| `POST`, `GET /api/v1/applyChangeSet[/{id}]` | a persisted task resource: `201` with `Location` and its `state` (1.4) |
| `GET /api/v1/deadDelivery[/{id}]` | dead deliveries, without payload values, with 10.3 queries and 10.4 paging (4.6) |
| `POST /api/v1/retryDeadDelivery`, `POST /api/v1/discardDeadDelivery` | stored `core.dead_delivery_action` tasks: `201` with `Location` to their one ordinary collection, `coreDeadDeliveryAction` (4.6) |
| `GET /api/v1/coreDeadDeliveryAction[/{id}[/history[/{changeId}]]]` | task records and their change history through the ordinary record routes; GET includes HEAD |
| `POST /api/v1/session`; `GET`, `DELETE /api/v1/session/current` | sign-in (`tokenDelivery`: `body` or `cookie`; a cookie sign-in without `STD-Client: web` is `400 InvalidRequest` and sets no cookie, decision 0018), the current session, sign-out |
| `POST /api/v1/hub`, `GET`, `DELETE /api/v1/hub/{id}` | event subscriptions (12.2) |
| `GET /api/v1/openapi.json` | the OpenAPI document, no session needed |

- The session cookie's path is `/api`. `STD-Client: web`, `STD-Schema-Revision`, `Cache-Control: no-store` (exclusion `X630-no-store` from TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §1.5.1's caching SHOULDs, D2) and the stale-request rules (6.2, 6.4) apply; API names are resolved at the request's revision R.
- Every resource shape answers a method it does not offer (`PUT` everywhere, `POST` on a record, `PATCH` on a collection or a session, any method no route serves, and so on) with `405` and an accurate `Allow` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §3.1 p.27, §3.3.3 p.31, §5.1 p.101]. `Allow` lists the shape's methods, `HEAD` wherever `GET` is, and `OPTIONS` everywhere. `OPTIONS` is `204` with that `Allow` and no body; `HEAD` answers as `GET` (status and headers, `ETag` and `Link` included) without a body [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §3.1 p.26: `HEAD` returns metadata only; RFC 9110 §9.3.2, §9.3.7]. The shape of the path alone decides `405` and `OPTIONS`, after the session is authenticated in the request transaction like every other route (an invalid, expired or revoked session is `401 Unauthenticated` without `Allow`; `/session` and `/openapi.json` need none) [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §1.6 pp.15-17, §3.1 p.26; review round 3, decision 0025] and before any table or record is looked up, so a caller gets the same answer whether the record is visible, hidden or missing [judgment]. An unknown path under `/api/v1` is a TMF `NotFound`.
- The shared `HttpApi` definition in `@std/api` is the contract: `/api/v1/openapi.json` is generated from it, the server checks every request body against its request schemas before reading it (a body it refuses is `400 InvalidRequest`), and the test client checks every `/api/v1` response of every suite against its response schemas for the status [judgment: the document and the server cannot drift].
- The session cookie moved from `Path=/api/v1` to `Path=/api` in Step 3: every cookie sign-in and sign-out also expires a cookie left at the old path, and a request carrying two session cookies is not authenticated by cookie (decision 0025).

### 10.2 Resource representation

- A record is one flat JSON object, not an envelope [checked: v5 `*_RES` schemas]:

```json
{
  "id": "01920c4e-7a3b-7c1d-9a2e-3f4b5c6d7e8f",
  "href": "https://std.example/api/v1/helpdeskIncident/01920c4e-7a3b-7c1d-9a2e-3f4b5c6d7e8f",
  "@type": "HelpdeskIncident",
  "@baseType": "HelpdeskTicket",
  "@schemaLocation": "https://std.example/api/v1/tableMetadata/helpdeskIncident/schema.json",
  "revision": 7,
  "creationDate": "2026-10-20T08:15:30.123Z",
  "createdBy": {
    "@type": "EntityRef",
    "@referredType": "CoreUser",
    "id": "…",
    "href": "…",
    "name": "Admin"
  },
  "number": "INC0001001",
  "shortDescription": "Mail is down",
  "assignedTo": {
    "@type": "EntityRef",
    "@referredType": "CoreUser",
    "id": "…",
    "href": "…",
    "name": "Alice Smith"
  },
  "estimate": "12.50",
  "withheld": ["customerEmail"]
}
```

- `id` and `href` are always present; `href` is the absolute URL of the record through its concrete class [checked: `Addressable`; `id` and `href` required in `TroubleTicket_RES`], from `STD_PUBLIC_URL`'s origin, else the request's `Host` (`http` for a loopback host, `https` otherwise) [judgment]. `@type` is always present [checked: v5 `Extensible` requires `@type`].
- **Attribute names** are lowerCamelCase [checked: every v5 schema]. A field's API name is its definition name split at runs of underscores, the parts after the first capitalized and joined (`short_description` → `shortDescription`, `due__date` → `dueDate`, `foo_` → `foo`) [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §2.5 pp.22-23]; table names derive the same way (10.1). The schema engine refuses a field whose API name equals the API name of another live field of the hierarchy, or a reserved name (the system attributes below, `href`, `version`, `withheld` and anything starting with `@`), with `DefinitionInvalid` problem `apiNameClash` [judgment: definition names stay snake_case so plain SQL stays readable (1.1); resolved question 9]; only the fields a change set creates, renames or makes live again are checked, so an older clash blocks nothing, and the API resolves neither of two clashing names. Filter text (`condition`), `fields`, `sort` and write bodies use API names.
- System fields: `revision` (the record's version counter, also the `ETag`), `creationDate`, `createdBy`, `lastUpdate`, `updatedBy` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P7 §2.1.3 p.18; P4 §3 pp.28-33: `version` names an entity version, so STD's counter is not `version`]; `class` becomes `@type`. `type` is reserved [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P7 §2.1.3 p.18]; the shipped platform field `core.field.type` is `fieldType`.
- **Empty attributes are left out**, never `null` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.1 p.39]; `withheld` tells a restricted attribute from an empty one, and `null` in a merge-patch still clears.
- **Polymorphism** [checked: `@type`, `@baseType`, `@schemaLocation` in v5 `Extensible`; v4.0.0 P2 §1 pp.3-5]: `@type` is the type name of the concrete class; `@baseType` is the type name of its parent class, absent on a root's own records [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P8 rules 3 and 6 to 9 pp.11-13]; `@schemaLocation` is the caller's JSON Schema of the concrete class (`/api/v1/tableMetadata/{table}/schema.json`, built from the same metadata as 6.3, so it names no field the caller cannot read). A caller who can see a record always knows its concrete class (3.4), so none of the three leaks a class name. Through a parent collection a read or list returns each record's concrete representation, its own fields under its access rules (Martin, 2026-10-22, D1; v4.0.0 P2 §1 p.4; 2.3), unless `fields` selects attributes; each concrete class's default selection is then in the request's stale-request scope (6.4), so a request stated at an older revision is `SchemaChanged` through the parent exactly when it is through the child, checked only for the classes of records the caller can see; the response's `Content-Location` names its URI through its concrete class, and every single resource answers `Link: <requested URI>; rel="self", <href>; rel="canonical"` [checked: v4.0.0 P2 §1 p.5, P3 §2.5 p.14]. A child class's `schema.json` leaves out inherited fields the caller cannot read there (exclusion `X630-hidden-base-fields`, D2).
- **References** are typed references when the caller knows the field's declared target class (`{"@type": "CoreUserRef", ...}`, `<TypeName>Ref`) and generic `EntityRef`s otherwise [checked: v5 `EntityRef` requires `id`, adds `name`, `@referredType`, `href`; TMF630 v5.0.0 P7 §1.4.3 p.15]: `{"@type", "id", "href", "@referredType", "name"}`. `@referredType` and `href` name the target's concrete class when the target is visible to the caller, otherwise the declared target class if the caller knows it (6.3), otherwise both are absent (exclusion `X630-referred-type-hidden`, Martin 2026-10-22, D2: information hiding wins over v4.0.0 P2 §4 p.25 and v5.0.0 P7 §1.4.2 p.14). `name` is the label under exactly the 3.6 rule. `schema.json` defines the same reference type for each reference attribute (`#/$defs/CoreUserRef` or `#/$defs/EntityRef`) and no type the caller does not know, so every resource validates against its `@schemaLocation`. This replaces `labels=true`; choice labels come from metadata. In writes only `id` is read; a non-null `@referredType` must name the field's target class or a descendant, else `InvalidReference`.
- `withheld` lists selected attributes withheld on this record (6.6). It is STD's extension of the record base type, not a TMF attribute [judgment: TMF has no "restricted" marker, and a restricted field must never look empty, Step 2 gate].
- Value shapes are 6.5's, unchanged (big integers and decimals stay strings).

### 10.3 Query parameters on lists and reads

| Parameter | Meaning | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `fields` | Comma-separated attribute names and one-hop paths (`assignedTo.email` adds that value to the EntityRef; `assignedTo.id` and `assignedTo.name` select the reference); `none` for the identity attributes only; `!name` leaves an attribute out of the default selection, and including and excluding one attribute is `409 FieldsConflict`; `id`, `href`, `@type`, `@baseType`, `@schemaLocation` and `revision` are always returned | [checked: TMF621 CP v5.0.0, API GET Operation Conformance p.7; TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.3 pp.50-57] |
| `<attr>=<v>` | `eq(attr, v)`; several attributes AND | [checked: TMF621 CP v5.0.0, Definitions for Filter p.7; TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.4 p.57] |
| `<attr>=<v1>,<v2>`, `<attr>=<v1>;<v2>` and `<attr>=<v1>&<attr>=<v2>` | `in(attr, [v1, v2])`; a backslash escapes `,`, `;` and itself in a value [judgment] | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.4 pp.57-58] |
| `<attr>.eq=`, `.gt=`, `.gte=`, `.lt=`, `.lte=`, and the URL-encoded forms `<attr>%3D=`, `%3E=`, `%3E%3D=`, `%3C=`, `%3C%3D=` | `eq`, `gt`, `ge`, `lt`, `le`; repeats are OR | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.4 pp.58-60] |
| `<ref>.id=`, `<ref>.<attr>=` | the reference itself, or a one-hop path (5.9, 3.6 masks) | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.4.1 dot notation; judgment for references] |
| `@type=<Type>` | `class` equals the class with that type name (2.3); `@type=A,B` is `in`. It is the only class predicate: `condition` cannot name the class (a class atom inside `and`, `or` or `not` is not expressible on this API; the old `filter`'s `class` path is gone), so a class predicate is ANDed to the whole query [judgment, decision 0025] | [judgment] |
| `.<name>=` | the attribute `<name>` even when `<name>` is a directive (`.sort=x`) | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.3] |
| `offset`, `limit` | offset paging; `limit` 1 to 1,000, default 100; a negative offset, a limit outside 1 to 1,000 is `400`; an offset past the end is an empty `200` | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.5.1 pp.75-77] |
| `after`, `before` | opaque cursors (STD's keyset cursor, 3.5); both together select the range between them | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.5.3 to §4.5.5] |
| `sort` | `sort=-cost,+name`; ascending by default, `-` reverses; one-hop paths are not supported yet | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.8 p.99] |
| `filter` | JSONPath filter (TMF630 Part 6): not supported in v0.1, `501 NotImplemented` (`feature` `jsonPathNotSupported`; exclusion E14) | [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P6 §4 p.40, §8 p.53; judgment to refuse, resolved question 13] |
| `condition` | STD's condition text syntax (Contracts 5, `@std/conditions`) with API names, ANDed with the TMF filters | STD extension [judgment] |
| `count` | `count=false` drops `X-Total-Count` on cursor pages; offset pages always carry it | STD extension [judgment; TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.5.1 p.77] |
| `allowedFor` | `<collection>/<attribute>`: a reference attribute's allowed-records condition, ANDed (decision 0020); refusals name what the request said | STD extension [judgment] |

- Mapping onto the condition AST (5.2): values are parsed from query text by the field type's input rules (6.5): a JSON number for integers (`5`), text for big integers and decimals, `true`/`false`, dates and date-times as in 6.5, choice keys, UUIDs. A date-only value on a date-time attribute is allowed [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.4 p.57]: equality means that UTC day, a comparison its first instant [judgment]. `<attr>=` with an empty value, an operator suffix the type does not support (5.4) and `.regex` (exclusion E17) are `InvalidFilter`; v5 has no `.exact`. TMF attribute filters compare text unmodified, so `name=alice` does not match `Alice` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §13.2.3 p.242; Martin, 2026-10-22, D4]; `condition` and the browser keep each field's configured case behavior (5.5), so a caller matches case-insensitively with `condition=name = "alice"`. The two compile separately; the shared compiler's semantics are unchanged. Every name resolves against the caller's visible schema, so a hidden attribute gives `UnknownField` exactly as a missing one (3.4). Repeated parameters for the same attribute and operator are OR; everything else is AND.
- A query parameter that is neither a directive nor an attribute filter is `InvalidRequest`; one shaped like an attribute filter resolves as one (`UnknownField`) [judgment: an unknown attribute must not be silently ignored, because an ignored filter widens the result].
- `offset` with `after` or `before` is `InvalidRequest`. Offset pages use the same `ORDER BY` with `OFFSET` and the same masking (3.5); STD makes no stability promise across changes for offset pages (the cursor gives that) [judgment]. A page asked for with `offset` is an offset page; any other page (the first one, or one asked for with a cursor) is a cursor page [judgment].
- A single read takes only `fields`; anything else is `InvalidRequest`.

### 10.4 List responses

- `200` with a JSON array of resources [checked: v5 list responses are arrays with `200`]. STD offers no range-header paging, so it never answers `206` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §3.3.1 p.30, §4.5.2 pp.81-84 keep `206` for it].
- `X-Result-Count`: the number of items in this response. `X-Total-Count`: the number matching the filter, computed with the page's `FROM` and `WHERE` (3.5), on every list unless `count=false` on a cursor page [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §1.5.5 p.15, §4.5.1 p.77; the TMF688 swagger lists].
- `Link` (RFC 8288) [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.5.1 p.77, §4.5.3 p.92]: `self`, `first`, `prev` (after the first page), `next` (when more follow) and `last` on offset pages; `self`, `next` (`after=<cursor>`, when more follow) and, on a page asked for with a cursor, `prev` (`before=<cursor>`) on cursor pages.
- History lists are `ChangeEntry` resources (`@baseType` `History`) [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §12 pp.206-238]: `{"@type": "ChangeEntry", "@baseType": "History", "id": <change ID>, "operationId", "revision", "action" (created, updated, deleted), "kind", "actor": EntityRef, "revisedBy" (the same), "occurredAt", "creationDate" (the same), "schemaRevision", "cause", "changes", "fields", "entity": EntityRef}`, filtered as in 3.7 (`since`, `until`, `actor`, `kind`, `field`) and on ChangeEntry attributes (`action`, `kind`, `revision` with `.gt`/`.gte`/`.lt`/`.lte`, `creationDate` likewise, `revisedBy.id`), selected with `fields`, paged with `offset`/`limit`, both count headers and `Link`; each entry has an `href`, and `GET /api/v1/{table}/{id}/history/{changeId}` returns one entry the caller may see, else `NotFound` (Martin, 2026-10-22, D3). Filters never read record values (3.7).
- History links [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §12.3 p.210, §12.3.3 pp.231-232, §12.4 p.238; RFC 5829]: every single-resource answer (read, create, update) adds `<href>/history; rel="version-history"` and the latest entry as `rel="latest-version"`; a history list adds `latest-version`, the current revision whatever the list's filters and page select (TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §12.4 p.238); one entry answers `self`, `version-history`, `latest-version`, and `predecessor-version` and `successor-version` where they exist. Every link is derived from the entries the caller may see (3.7), so none names an entry the caller cannot read, and a record the caller cannot see answers its identical `NotFound` with no links.
- History also accepts opaque `after`/`before` cursors over numeric record versions; backward pages keep ascending response order, and both cursors select their exclusive range. `offset` with a cursor is `InvalidRequest`. Existing history requests without cursors or `count` retain their offset navigation; explicit cursors or `count` without `offset` select cursor navigation (`self`, `next`, and `prev` when applicable). `count=false` omits totals only on these cursor pages; offset pages always return exact totals. Both original exclusive range bounds survive forward and backward navigation inside the opaque cursors, without changing the total matching the filters. An opposite-direction existence check omits `prev` or `next` when no matching entries precede or follow the page. Filters, field selection and the requested limit survive navigation [judgment: preserve existing history links while adding keyset paging; TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.5.1 pp.75-77, §4.5.3 pp.85-92; decision 0040].

### 10.5 Writes

- `POST /api/v1/{table}`: `201` with the resource and `Location` [checked: v5 `201`]. `@type` is optional and defaults to `{table}`; if given it must name a class of subtree(table) the caller knows, and the body resolves against that class's fields, so a client can create a child-class record through the parent collection [judgment: TMF polymorphic create; this widens 2.2 item 5, and the access checks run on the class created]. `@baseType` and `@schemaLocation` in a body are ignored when they match the created class's, else `InvalidRequest`. `id`, `href`, `revision`, `version`, `withheld` and the audit attributes are `ReadOnlyField` (6.7). The response's `fields` query selects its attributes, as for a read [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §4.3].
- `PATCH /api/v1/{table}/{id}` takes `application/merge-patch+json` (RFC 7396), and `application/json` as the same (deprecated in v5, gone in v6 [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §3.2 p.28]), and `application/json-patch+json` (RFC 6902) [checked: TMF621 UG v5.0.1 p.73, CP v5.0.0 p.9: merge-patch mandatory, JSON Patch optional]. Any other media type, `application/json-patch-query+json` included, is `415` with `Accept-Patch` [judgment, resolved question 13]. A malformed JSON Patch is `400` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §8.5 p.129].
  - Merge-patch is the 6.7 update body: an absent attribute is unchanged, `null` clears. For a json field the patch value is merged into the stored value by RFC 7396 [judgment: TMF clients expect merge semantics on nested objects]; for a reference only the EntityRef's `id` counts.
  - JSON Patch operations apply in order to the caller's readable image of the record as locked by the save: the pipeline resolves every path's attribute name and runs the stale check (6.4) at the request's revision first (4.1 step 1), then locks the root row and checks visibility and the version (step 2), and only then builds the image from the locked state and applies the operations; the result becomes the update body for step 3 onwards. So a `test` or a `copy` source always sees the version the write applies to, with or without `If-Match`. The RFC 7396 merge into a json field is computed the same way, after the lock. A path is `/<attribute>` or a pointer into a json field. `test`, `copy` and `move` read their source, so a source the caller cannot read is `UnknownField`, as in 3.4. A failed `test` is `409 PatchTestFailed` and writes nothing. An operation on a read-only attribute is `ReadOnlyField`.
  - Non-patchable: `id`, `href`, `revision`, the audit attributes, auto-numbers and read-only fields [checked: TMF621 UG v5.0.1 pp.73-74; TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §5.2 pp.101-103]. An unchanged `@type`, `@baseType` or `@schemaLocation` in a merge-patch is accepted, a different one is `ReadOnlyField` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §5.2 p.103: clients send `@type`].
  - A JSON Patch that does not apply to the image (a failed `test`, or a path an operation reads or removes that is not there; empty attributes are not in the image, so set one with `add`) is `409 PatchTestFailed` naming the operation and writes nothing. A `test` of a reference compares its `id` only [judgment].
  - The result is `200` with the resource and `ETag: "<revision>"`.
- `DELETE /api/v1/{table}/{id}`: `204` with no body [checked: v5 `204`]; a `DELETE` with a body is `400` [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §7 p.126].
- **Version checks.** `PATCH` and `DELETE` require `If-Match: "<revision>"` (`428 VersionRequired`) [judgment: STD's generic API never writes last-wins, 6.7; the TMF Open APIs make it optional, 11.1], and every single-resource response carries `ETag: "<revision>"`. A stale `If-Match` is `412` (`VersionConflict`, with the current `ETag`) [checked: TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §1.5.3 p.14; Martin, 2026-10-22, M2].
- The save pipeline (4.1) is unchanged underneath: the stale check (6.4) runs on the attributes the patch names, before validation, access and version checks; a JSON Patch's scope is every attribute its operations touch or read. Only the evaluation of the patch moves after the lock, as above (`UpdateInput.evaluate`); the record service takes no expected version for the TMF APIs, and the patch is evaluated the same way.
- Writes to definition tables through `/api/v1/{table}` are change sets, as on every API (1.4): the body uses API names, `PATCH` takes a merge patch with `If-Match` (a JSON Patch is `415`), `DELETE` answers `204`, and the answer is the definition as the caller reads it at the change set's revision. `POST /api/v1/applyChangeSet` applies several operations as one change set (`201` with `Location`, or `200` with `state: unchanged` when nothing changed) and `GET /api/v1/applyChangeSet/{id}` serves its journal entry to callers that may read `core.schema_change` (decision 0025, API consolidation).

### 10.6 Errors

- Every error body is a TMF `Error` [checked: v5 `Error` requires `code` and `reason`; adds `message`, `status`, `referenceError`, and `@type` from `Extensible`; TMF630 v5.0.0 P1 §3.4 pp.34-35]: `{"@type": "<_tag>", "@baseType": "Error", "@schemaLocation": "/api/v1/openapi.json#/components/schemas/<_tag>", "code": "<_tag>", "reason": "<fixed text per _tag>", "status": "<HTTP status as a string>", …}`. STD's details stay as extension attributes of the subtype: `fieldErrors` (`ValidationFailed`), `currentVersion` (`VersionConflict`), `requestRevision`, `currentRevision`, `changes` (`SchemaChanged`), `problems` (`DefinitionInvalid`), `issues` (`InvalidFilter`) [judgment: TMF630 extends by subtyping].
- `message` is a fixed template per tag, never a value or Postgres text (3.8); `InvalidRequest` names its check, and `RuleRefused` and `InvalidTransition` carry the record rule's fixed message (4.10). The identical-response rules of 3.4 and 3.8 (`UnknownTable`, `UnknownField`, `NotFound`, `InvalidReference`) hold byte for byte. A resource outside the caller's scope or deleted answers this same 404, not TMF630's 403 or 410 (exclusion `X630-identical-404`, Martin 2026-10-22, D2).
- Status codes are those of 3.8, except `VersionConflict`, which is `412` (10.5); `PatchTestFailed` and `FieldsConflict` are 409, `MethodNotAllowed` 405 (with `Allow`), `UnsupportedMediaType` 415 (with `Accept-Patch`), `NotImplemented` 501. `SchemaBusy` and `SchemaLockTimeout` keep `Retry-After`.

### 10.7 What changes for clients

- Clients of the Step 0 to 2 API are not supported (decision 0021, decision 56); the release-compatibility step upgrades a database the previous release built and runs this branch's quickstart against it (architecture, "Release compatibility").
- The web app, `examples/quickstart.sh`, `examples/helpdesk-sign-in.sh` and the typed client (`@std/api/client`, derived from the shared definition) speak this API.
- Request schemas decode an absent new property as its default (decision 0017).

### Acceptance tests

- `tmf630-conformance`: one test per rule in 10.1 to 10.6, against the acme and helpdesk fixtures: paths, the representation (every attribute name in lowerCamelCase, `@type` on every object, `href` dereferences to the same resource), `fields`, each filter form and its AST, offset and cursor paging with both count headers, `sort`, polymorphic create through a parent, merge-patch including a nested json merge, every JSON Patch operation including a failed `test`, `204` on delete, `ETag` on reads and writes, the Error shape for every `_tag`.
- `tmf-filter-equivalence`: property test. For generated TMF queries over every condition-capable type, the IDs and order equal those of the same query written in the `condition` syntax.
- The Contracts 3 tests named for Stage 0 and Step 1 (`contracts3` and the suites they name), `error-fuzz`, `invisible-identity` and the stale-request tests run against this API.
- `api-name-clash`: a field whose API name equals a live field's in its hierarchy (`size_1` next to `size1`; a field renamed `dueDate` next to `due_date`) is refused with `apiNameClash`, and so are `href`, `withheld` and `@x`; tables whose API names clash with each other or a fixed resource (`table.metadata`) are refused too; a clash older than the rule blocks no unrelated change set.
- `api-contract`: every documented endpoint answers a success and an error whose status and body the shared definition documents (the test client checks every API response of every suite the same way), and a request body the shared request schemas refuse is `400 InvalidRequest`.
- `json-patch-race`: through the record service without a version (as the TMF APIs call it, 11.1), two concurrent patches, each with `test` on the same value and a `replace` of it: exactly one succeeds and the other is `PatchTestFailed` with nothing written; a `copy` whose source a concurrent save changes copies the value of the version it is applied to. Through the API, where `If-Match` is required, two concurrent patches on one revision give one `200` and one `412`. TMF621 `PATCH` reuses the record service's path (3T).

## 11. TMF Open API mappings (Steps 3 and 4)

### 11.1 Common rules

- **Pinned versions** [checked: the TM Forum Open API directory and its document bucket, 2026-10-22, decision 0021]. Moving a pin is its own PR, with its kit. `packages/tmf/specs/pins.json` holds these pins with each document's source commit and SHA-256 and each kit's zip URL, SHA-256, image and default URL (Contracts 15.1):

| API | Version | Base path | Resources STD serves | CTK | Step |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TMF621 Trouble Ticket | 5.0.1 | `/tmf-api/troubleTicket/v5` | `troubleTicket`, `troubleTicketSpecification`, `hub` | `tmf621-v5.0.0-ctk-1.0.1` | 3 |
| TMF688 Event | 4.0.0 (pre-production) | `/tmf-api/event/v4` | `topic`, `topic/{id}/event`, `topic/{id}/hub`, `hub` | none: OpenAPI contract tests | 3 |
| TMF632 Party | 5.0.0 | `/tmf-api/partyManagement/v5` | `individual`, `organization`, `hub` | `tmf632-v5.0.0-ctk-1.0.1` | 4 |
| TMF669 Party Role | 5.0.0 | `/tmf-api/partyRoleManagement/v5` | `partyRole`, `partyRoleSpecification`, `hub` | `tmf669-v5.0.0-ctk-1.0.1` | 4 |
| TMF672 User Role Permission | 5.1.0 | `/tmf-api/rolesAndPermissionsManagement/v5` | `permissionSpecification`, `permissionSpecificationSet`, `permissionSet`, `checkPermission`, `hub` | `tmf672-v5.1.0-ctk-1.0.1` | 4 |
| TMF638 Service Inventory | 5.0.0 | `/tmf-api/serviceInventoryManagement/v5` | `service`, `hub` | `tmf638-v5.0.0-ctk-1.0.1` | 4 |
| TMF639 Resource Inventory | 5.0.0 | `/tmf-api/resourceInventory/v5` | `resource`, `hub` | `tmf639-v5.0.0-ctk-1.0.1` | 4 |
| TMF641 Service Ordering | 4.2.0 (no stable v5) | `/tmf-api/serviceOrdering/v4` | `serviceOrder`, `cancelServiceOrder`, `hub` | `tmf641-v4.2.0-ctk-1.0.0` | 4 |
| TMF622 Product Ordering | 5.0.0 | `/tmf-api/productOrderingManagement/v5` | `productOrder`, `cancelProductOrder`, `hub` | `tmf622-v5.0.0-ctk-1.0.1` | 4 |

The documents' `servers` entries are inconsistent (`https://serverRoot`, `{apiRoot}` with a default, a full path, and none of the v5 defaults contains `/tmf-api`), and the user guides' examples disagree with them too, so a base path follows the pinned kit's default `url` in the kit's `config.json` [checked: the `config.json` of each of the eight pinned kits; TMF688, which has no kit, from its swagger `basePath` `/tmf-api/event/v4/`; decision 0026, F638-04, F632-01, F688-07]. TMF638's kit uses `/tmf-api/serviceInventoryManagement/v5`, not the `serviceInventory` of its guide's examples. A kit that disagrees after a pin moves wins. The pinned OpenAPI documents (Apache-2.0) are vendored under `packages/tmf/specs/` with their source URL and SHA-256, and `@std/tmf`'s tests check that each base path equals its kit's default `url`.

- **One enforcement point.** A TMF API is an adapter over the record service: every read is a generic read of the mapped classes, every write a save or nested saves in one request transaction (4.1), as the calling user. No mapping reads or writes a table directly, and no mapping uses `WriteAccess.allowAll`. So access rules, masks, history, the outbox and stale checks apply unchanged, and a TMF API can never show or accept more than the generic API would. An attribute the caller cannot read is absent from the resource, and a filter on it is `UnknownField` (3.4) rendered as a TMF `Error`.
- **Where mappings live.** A mapping is code in `@std/tmf` (plain package: attribute and state maps, query translation, event rendering, payload shapes) plus its routes in `server/http/tmf/`; it names fields by the stable IDs of the shipped sets, so renames never break it. The classes come from shipped sets: `core` (parties), `helpdesk` (tickets, changes, requests), `cmdb` (configuration items). A mapping covers its class and every descendant, local ones included.
- **`@type` and local classes.** A shipped class renders the TMF type its mapping names. A local child class of a mapped class renders `@type` = its type name, the UpperCamelCase form of its qualified name that `/api/v1` uses (`local.vip_incident` → `LocalVipIncident`; decision 0021, decision 37), `@baseType` = the TMF type of its nearest mapped ancestor, and `@schemaLocation` = its JSON Schema, which adds its local attributes (with API names, 10.2) to the pinned TMF schema [checked: the v5 extension pattern; judgment on names]. `POST` with such an `@type` creates that class.
- **Embedded arrays.** A TMF array either maps onto STD reference fields and child records (when its items name STD records: notes, related parties, order items, relationships) or is stored whole in a `json` field (value objects STD does not interpret: characteristics, contact media, external references, prices). Each mapping table says which. Child records are **aggregate parts** of the parent (4.9): their reference to it is its class's owner reference, so every write of a part, through any surface (STD's API, TMF APIs, imports, hooks and rules), locks the aggregate and changes its version, history and events in the same transaction [resolved question 20]. A TMF write saves the owner's own attributes at most once and its parts as needed, and the owner is then persisted once more if a part or derived state changed (4.9). So one write is one `aggregateOperation` that may commit two owner versions: history keeps the intermediate version, the per-save outbox hooks may enqueue a delivery for each, and the response's `ETag` is the final version [precised, decision 0033]. The TMF `DELETE` deletes the parts, innermost first and each authorized for the caller, then the owner; a deleted owner is not finalized (4.9). A generic `DELETE` of an owner that has parts is still `Referenced` (3.10). A merge-patch follows RFC 7396 strictly for an array of parts: the array replaces the old one, so a part it omits is deleted, a part whose `id` matches is updated, and an item without a known `id` is created, each as a part save inside the aggregate save (4.9), each authorized for the caller (Martin, 2026-10-22; decision 0021, decision 41). JSON Patch operates on the same array.
- **Versions.** TMF responses carry `ETag: "<parent version>"`. `If-Match` is optional on TMF writes: present and stale, the write answers the version-conflict status the pinned OpenAPI document declares for that operation, with the current `ETag`: `409` for TMF621's `PATCH` and `DELETE`, which declare `409` and not `412` [checked: TMF621 OAS `/troubleTicket/{id}` patch and delete responses] (Martin, 2026-10-22: on TMF routes the pinned document wins; decision 0021, decision 55, amending decision 36 for TMF routes). `/api/v1`, STD's own TMF630 API, keeps `412` with the current `ETag` and `428` for a missing `If-Match` [TMF630 v5.0.0 Part 1 §1.5.3 p.14]; absent, the write applies to the version it locks [judgment: the CTKs and TMF clients send no precondition; exclusion E27, accepted; the generic API keeps it required, 10.5].
- **Statuses on TMF routes** (Martin, 2026-10-22; decision 0021, decision 52). A TMF route answers only statuses its pinned OpenAPI document declares for that operation: `ValidationFailed` and `InvalidReference` are `400` with the STD tag in the `Error`'s `code`; an unsupported PATCH media type is `400` `InvalidRequest`; `429` on hub registration (12.2) is a recorded deviation (exclusion E29, accepted), and so are `413` and `503` from the request-body budgets (exclusions E30 and E31, accepted 2026-10-23; request body handling). `tmf-oas-contract` fails on any other undeclared status. STD's own API keeps the statuses of 3.8 and 10.6.
- **Queries, paging, errors, hubs** follow 10.3, 10.4, 10.6 and 12, with TMF attribute names. Filters on an attribute stored as json or as an array are `InvalidFilter` reason `attributeNotFilterable` [judgment: the condition AST has no "any element" operator; the conformance runs record whether a kit needs one; exclusion E16]. Filtering on sub-resources is optional in every conformance profile checked [checked: TMF621 CP v5.0.0, API GET Operation Conformance p.7; TMF688 CP v4.0.0 pp.9-10; TMF632, TMF669 and TMF672 profiles (decision 0026, F632-05)].
- **Unsupported resources** of an API (for example TMF641's `serviceOrderSpecification`) answer `404` with a TMF `Error` and are listed in the API's conformance note; a kit that requires one is a gap, not a pass.
- **Conformance gate.** CI downloads each pinned kit by URL, checks its SHA-256 and the image digest, never commits it, writes its `config.json` (base URL on the `tmf` Docker network, a bearer token for the `tmf_ctk` test user, payloads naming seeded STD records), runs the kit's compose service directly (not `run.sh`, which opens a browser), and decides from the report alone, never from the container's exit code: the TMF621 kit's entrypoint runs `npm start || true` and so exits 0 when tests fail [checked for TMF621's kit only; the runner trusts no kit's exit code]. The runner (`tooling/ctk/run.ts`) passes a kit only when all of these hold:
  - `reports/index.json` exists, parses, and was written by this run (its start time is after the run's start, and the reports directory was empty before);
  - `ctk_version.txt` names the pinned image;
  - `stats.failures`, `stats.pending` and `stats.skipped` are 0, and `stats.passes` equals `stats.tests`;
  - every expected mandatory case for that kit passed: the requirements matrix (15) lists the kit's mandatory test titles at the pinned version, and each must appear among the passed tests by exact title, compared normalized: the kit's titles embed the configured base URL and runs of tabs and new lines, so the URL is recorded as `{url}` and each run of white space as one space [precised, decision 0033].

  The runner also checks the image's repository digest against the pin (`ctk.imageDigest` in `pins.json`) before it runs it [precised, decision 0033].

  Every TMF response in every test also validates against the pinned OpenAPI document (`tmf-oas-contract`). Reports are kept as CI artifacts, so a certification submission can reuse them. Version labels can differ: the TMF621 OpenAPI document is v5.0.1 while its kit and its conformance profile's cover say 5.0.0 (`tmf621-v5.0.0-ctk-1.0.1`; TMF621B v5.0.0) [checked: TMF621 UG v5.0.1 release history p.106, where 5.0.1 only fixes examples]; the pin records both.

### 11.2 TMF621 Trouble Ticket over ITIL incident and problem (Step 3)

The helpdesk set v3 (decision 0021) rebuilds `helpdesk.ticket` as the TroubleTicket base, `helpdesk.incident` and the new `helpdesk.problem` extending it. Notes are `helpdesk.ticket_note` records and other related parties `helpdesk.ticket_party` records, both referencing their ticket.

Operations [checked: TMF621 CP v5.0.0, TroubleTicket Mandatory Operations p.6 and the GET, POST, PATCH and DELETE sections pp.7-10]: `GET /troubleTicket`, `GET /troubleTicket/{id}` (`404` when absent), `POST /troubleTicket` (`201`) and `DELETE /troubleTicket/{id}` (`204`) are mandatory; `PATCH` is optional (`200`, merge-patch mandatory when offered), and STD serves it. TroubleTicket is the only mandatory resource; `hub` and `troubleTicketSpecification` are optional [checked: CP p.2]. Every ticket, however it was created (TMF621, `/api/v1`, a form, an import), renders the attributes the profile makes mandatory in responses, non-blank: `@type`, `href`, `description`, `severity` and `ticketType` [checked: CP pp.2-3]. So `description` is required on `helpdesk.ticket` itself, `impact` always has a value, and `ticketType` always renders from the class or `ticket_type` (`tmf621-mandatory-output`).

| TMF621 attribute | STD | Read-only | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `id`, `href` | `id` | yes |  |
| `@type` | `TroubleTicket` for the shipped classes; a local class's own name (11.1) | yes | [judgment: the kit and TMF clients post `TroubleTicket`; the ITIL class is carried by `ticketType`] |
| `ticketType` | the concrete class: `incident`, `problem`; any other value creates a `helpdesk.ticket` and is stored in `ticket_type` | no; a value that would change the concrete class (to or from `incident` or `problem`) is `ValidationFailed`, any other value updates `ticket_type` (decision 0021, decision 40) | Required on create [checked: TMF621 CP v5.0.0 p.8; UG v5.0.1 pp.69-70; OAS `TroubleTicket_FVO`]. Free text in TMF621 [checked: UG p.8] |
| `name` | `short_description` (display field) | no | When absent on create, a shipped before-write rule fills it from the first line of `description`, cut to 160 code points |
| `description` | `description` | no | Required on create [checked: as `ticketType`] |
| `severity` | `impact`: `Critical` ↔ 1, `Major` ↔ 2, `Minor` ↔ 3 (case-insensitive); any other non-blank value is stored as given in `severity_label` and leaves `impact` as it is (its default, 3, on create) (decision 0021, decision 39) | no | Required on create [checked: as `ticketType`]. Free text in TMF621, with these three as examples [checked: OAS, no enum; UG p.8]. A recognized value sets `impact` and clears `severity_label` in the same write. Any write that changes `impact` without naming `severity_label` (a generic write, a recognized TMF severity, a rule) clears it, by the shipped `derive` rule `clear-severity-label` (`changed(impact)`, `set` `severity_label` null with `keepSupplied`). Reads render `severity_label` when set, else the label of `impact` |
| `priority` | `priority` label: `Critical`, `High`, `Medium`, `Low`, `Planning` (1 to 5); any other non-blank value is stored as given in `priority_label` and leaves `priority` unchanged | no | Free text in TMF621 and patchable [checked: UG pp.8, 73-74]. The impact × urgency rules set it when impact or urgency changes, and an explicit `priority` in the same write wins (14.2; decision 0021, decision 53). A recognized label sets `priority` and clears `priority_label` in the same write. Any change of `priority` without `priority_label` named (an explicit generic write, a recognized label, the matrix rules) clears it, by the shipped `derive` rule `clear-priority-label`, ordered after the matrix rules (`changed(priority)`, `set` `priority_label` null with `keepSupplied`); so an unrecognized label written together with an impact change stays and is rendered, and a later impact change that moves `priority` clears it. Reads render `priority_label` when set, else the label of `priority` |
| `status` | `status` (and `hold_reason`), table below | no | Writes follow the state model (14.2, 14.3) |
| `statusChangeDate`, `statusChangeReason` | `status_changed_at` (set by the pipeline rule on every status change), `status_reason` | date only |  |
| `statusChangeHistory[]` | the status changes in the record's history (3.7); a written value is stored as given in json `status_change_history` and rendered before the changes recorded after the write [judgment] | no | Absent when the caller cannot read `status`. Patchable in TMF621 (decision 0021, decision 53) |
| `creationDate`, `lastUpdate` | `created_at`, `updated_at` | yes |  |
| `requestedResolutionDate`, `expectedResolutionDate`, `resolutionDate` | `requested_resolution_at`, `expected_resolution_at`, `resolved_at` (set on entering `resolved` unless the write gives it) | no | `resolutionDate` is patchable in TMF621 (decision 0021, decision 53) |
| `channel` | `channel` choice: key ↔ `ChannelRef.id`, label ↔ `name` | no |  |
| `note[]` | `helpdesk.ticket_note`: `author` (reference to `core.user`, rendered as its display name), `date` (= `created_at`), `text` | no | A merge-patch `note` array replaces the old one (11.1, strict RFC 7396: decision 0021, decision 41), so notes it omits are deleted; `id` is the `helpdesk.ticket_note` ID |
| `relatedParty[]` | role `reporter` ↔ `caller` (reference to `core.party`); role `assignee` ↔ `assigned_to` (`core.user`, rendered as its individual, 11.3); other roles ↔ `helpdesk.ticket_party` (`party`, `role`) | no | Writes resolve `partyOrPartyRole.id` to the party, or for `assignee` to the user whose individual it is. Each item renders `{"@type": "RelatedPartyRefOrPartyRoleRef", role, partyOrPartyRole: {"@type": "PartyRef", id, name, "@referredType"}}` (11.3 for `href`); a write without `role` or `partyOrPartyRole.id` is `ValidationFailed` [checked: TMF621 CP v5.0.0 p.3]. Roles are free text in TMF621; `reporter` and `assignee` are STD's names |
| `troubleTicketRelationship[]` | `relationshipType` `causedBy` ↔ an incident's `problem` (reference to `helpdesk.problem`); `parent` ↔ `parent_incident` | no | Each item renders `@type` `TroubleTicketRelationship`, `id` and `relationshipType` [checked: TMF621 CP v5.0.0 p.4]. `relationshipType` is free text [checked: OAS, no enum], so other types are kept as given in json `ticket_relationship` |
| `relatedEntity[]` | role `affectedConfigurationItem` ↔ `configuration_item` (reference to `cmdb.ci`, from helpdesk v4 in Step 4); role `change` ↔ `caused_by_change` (Step 4); anything else kept as given in json `related_entity` | no |  |
| `troubleTicketCharacteristic[]` | `urgency` ↔ `urgency` (1 to 3), `majorIncident` ↔ `major_incident`, `holdReason` ↔ `hold_reason`, `resolutionCode` ↔ `resolution_code`, `resolutionNotes` ↔ `resolution_notes`; problems: `knownError`, `workaround`, `rootCause`; any other characteristic kept in json `characteristic` | per field | Typed as the v5 characteristic subtypes |
| `externalIdentifier[]` | first item `{owner: "STD", externalIdentifierType: "number", id: <number>}` (read-only), then json `external_identifier` | the first item |  |
